### Root beer

Root beer is a North American beverage traditionally made using the root bark of the sassafras tree Sassafras albidum or the sarsaparilla vine Smilax ornata (also used to make a soft drink called sarsaparilla) as the primary flavor.

- From: Pennsylvania, United States
- Origin (Wikidata): North America
- Since: 1840-
- Ingredients (Wikidata): Smilax ornata

Fun facts:

- (history) Root beer has been sold in confectionery stores since at least the 1840s, and written recipes for root beer have been documented since the 1830s.
- (culture) Safrole, the aromatic oil found in sassafras roots and bark that gave traditional root beer its distinctive flavor, was banned in commercially mass-produced foods and drugs by the FDA in 1960.
- (origin) Root beer comes from North America.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (history) Hires developed his root tea made from sassafras in 1875, debuted a commercial version of root beer at the Philadelphia Centennial Exposition in 1876, and began selling his extract.
- (history) Since safrole, a key component of sassafras, was banned by the US Food and Drug Administration in 1960 due to its carcinogenicity, most commercial root beers have been flavored using artificial sassafras flavoring, but a few (e.g.
- (history) Root beer was combined with soda as early as the 1850s; at that time it was sold as a syrup rather than a ready-made beverage.
- (history) By 1893, root beer was distributed widely across the United States.
- (history) One of Hires's early competitors was Barq's, which began selling its sarsaparilla-based root beer in 1898 and was labeled simply as "Barq's".
- (history) In 1919, Roy Allen opened his root-beer stand in Lodi, California, which led to the development of A&W Root Beer.
- (history) Root beer dates from 1840-.
- (culture) Not all traditional or commercial root beers were sassafras-based.
- (history) In 1886, Hires began to bottle a beverage made from his famous extract.

### Saffron

Saffron is a spice derived from the sterile autumn-blooming flower of Crocus sativus, commonly known as the "saffron crocus".

- From: Pennsylvania, United States

Fun facts:

- (history) The English word saffron is a borrowing from French, first found in a Middle English text written around 1200 as saffran.
- (origin) Saffron originated in Iran especially in the Khorasan region descended from wild relatives in the eastern Mediterranean and later spread to Kashmir, the Mediterranean region, and Asia Minor.
- (about) C. sativus is probably a triploid form of Crocus cartwrightianus, which is also known as "wild saffron".

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (origin) Saffron originated from the older زر پران zarparān, meaning 'gold-strung' in reference to the flower's golden filaments or the rich golden hue it imparts as a spice.
- (origin) Saffron likely originated in Iran, Greece, Mesopotamia, or Kashmir.
- (about) Not to be confused with Saffron.
- (about) Its recorded history is attested as early as a 7th-century BC Assyrian botanical treatise.
- (culture) Additional historical uses include traditional medicine, dyeing, perfumery, and religious rituals.

### Arizona cheese crisp

An Arizona cheese crisp (simply cheese crisp in the region) is an open-faced, flour tortilla covered in shredded cheese.

- Named after: Arizona

- (about) Cheese crisps are served as appetizers at most Phoenix-area and Tucson-area Mexican restaurants, but are rarely found outside the state, other than places popular with Arizonan tourists such as Puerto Peñasco and San Diego.

### Atole

Atole, also known as atolli, atol and atol de elote, is a traditional hot masa-based beverage of Mexican origin.

- Origin (Wikidata): Mexico
- Ingredients (Wikidata): drinking water, milk, maize, panela

Fun facts:

- (about) Chocolate atole is known as champurrado or simply atole.
- (origin) Atole comes from Mexico.
- (about) Many Classic Maya painted vessels feature a genre of inscriptions known as the "dedicatory formula" or the "primary standard sequence" (PSS) and the two main ingredients mentioned in the contents section of the PSS were cacao and atole.

### Bizcochito

The bizcochito or biscochito is a New Mexican crisp butter cookie made with lard, flavored with sugar, cinnamon, and anise.

- From: Southwestern US, United States
- Ingredients (Wikidata): sugar, cinnamon, butter, lard, anise oil

Fun facts:

- (history) The roots of this pastry date back as far as the Battle of Puebla in 1862, where French Emperor Maximilian was overthrown by the Mexicans.
- (about) The cookie was developed in New Mexico over the centuries from the first Spanish colonists of what was then known as Santa Fe de Nuevo México.
- (culture) The dough is rolled thin and cut into the shape of the fleur-de-lis, the Christian cross, a star, or a circle, symbolizing the moon.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (about) This date is now famously recognized in the United States as Cinco de Mayo, literally the "Fifth of May".
- (culture) Biscochitos are commonly served during celebrations such as wedding receptions, baptisms, and religious (especially Catholic) holidays, and frequently during the Christmas season.

### Chocolate milk

Chocolate milk is a type of flavoured milk made by mixing cocoa powder with milk (either dairy or plant-based).

- From: Southwestern US, United States
- Ingredients (Wikidata): milk, sugar, Theobroma cacao, cocoa powder

Fun facts:

- (about) The Natural History Museum of London lists Anglo-Irish botanist Hans Sloane as the inventor of drinking chocolate with milk.
- (history) According to historian James Delbourgo, the Jamaicans were brewing "a hot beverage brewed from shavings of freshly harvested cacao, boiled with milk and cinnamon" as far back as 1494.
- (origin) The first domesticated cattle were introduced to the Caribbean in 1493 by Christopher Columbus.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (history) In the late 19th century, Swiss entrepreneur Daniel Peter developed a solid dehydrated version of chocolate milk so that it could be easily portioned, transported and conserved (fresh milk was rare in the cities).
- (about) Not to be confused with milk chocolate.
- (origin) Reciprocally, cocoa was introduced in Europe in the 1520s.

### Frito pie

Frito pie is a dish popular in the Midwestern, Southeastern, and Southwestern United States, whose basic ingredients are chili, cheese, and corn chips (traditionally Fritos, hence the name).

- From: Southwestern US, United States
- Origin (Wikidata): United States
- Ingredients (Wikidata): chili

Fun facts:

- (inspiration) Another story says that true Frito pie originated only in the 1960s with Teresa Hernández, who worked at the Woolworth's lunch counter in Santa Fe, New Mexico.
- (history) Charles Doolin and his Frito Company were early investors in Disneyland, which opened Casa de Fritos restaurant in Disneyland in 1955. "Frito Chili Pie" appears on the 1950s menu.
- (origin) Frito pie comes from the United States.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (inspiration) The recipe may have been invented by Daisy Doolin, the mother of Frito Company founder Charles Elmer Doolin and the first person to use Fritos as an ingredient in cooking, or by Mary Livingston, Doolin's executive secretary.

### Guacamole

Guacamole, sometimes informally shortened to guac in the United States, is an avocado-based dip, spread, or salad first developed in Mexico.

- From: Southwestern US, United States
- Origin (Wikidata): Mexico
- Ingredients (Wikidata): table salt, jalapeño, avocado, lime juice, tomato, cilantro

Fun facts:

- (history) The rising consumption of guacamole is most likely due to the U.S. government lifting a ban on avocado imports in the 1990s and the growth of the U.S. Latino population.
- (about) On November 20, 2022, Peribán, Mexico, achieved the Guinness World Record for the largest serving of guacamole.
- (origin) Guacamole comes from Mexico.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (about) Avocado seeds were first found in the Tehuacan Valley of Mexico around 9,000–10,000 years ago (7000–8000 BCE) and had been domesticated by various Mesoamerican groups by 5000 BCE.
- (history) In the early 1900s, avocados frequently went by the name alligator pear.

### Jalapeño

The jalapeño ( HA(H)L-ə-PEE-nyoh, -⁠PAY-, Spanish: [xalaˈpeɲo] ) is a medium-sized chili pepper pod type cultivar of the species Capsicum annuum.

- From: Southwestern US, United States

Fun facts:

- (about) Contrary to popular belief, the jalapeño pepper is not grown in or around Xalapa, but Vicente Jiménez invented the process of pickling and canning the peppers in Xalapa.
- (history) The jalapeño is a Mexican chili but was designated by the Texas Legislature as the official "State Pepper of Texas" in 1995.
- (history) Jalapeños were included as food on the Space Shuttle as early as 1982.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (about) Guinness World Records recognizes Alfredo Hernandes for the most jalapeños eaten in a minute: 16, on 17 September 2006 at the La Costeña Feel the Heat Challenge in Chicago, Illinois.
- (about) Patrick Bertoletti holds the Major League Eating jalapeño records at 275 pickled jalapeños in 8 minutes on 1 May 2011, and 191 pickled jalapeños in 6.5 minutes on 16 September 2007 in the 'Short-Form'.
- (history) The official mascot of the 1986 FIFA World Cup was Pique, a jalapeño pepper, characteristic of Mexican cuisine, with a moustache, a Colimote sombrero, and Mexican football team colors.
- (about) The jalapeño is variously named huachinango, for the ripe red jalapeño, and chile gordo (meaning "fat chili pepper") also known as cuaresmeño.
- (about) Joaquín Guzmán, also known as "El Chapo", the leader of the Sinaloa Cartel, operated a cannery in Guadalajara producing "Comadre Jalapeños" in order to ship cocaine to the US.

### Salsa (food)

A salsa is any of a variety of sauces used as condiments for tacos and other Mexican and Mexican-American foods, and as dips for tortilla chips.

- From: Southwestern US, United States
- Origin (Wikidata): Mexico
- Ingredients (Wikidata): onion, tomato, chili pepper

Fun facts:

- (history) In the 1980s, tomato-based Mexican-style salsas gained in popularity.
- (about) The use of salsa as a table dip was popularized by Mexican restaurants in the United States.
- (origin) Salsa comes from Mexico.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (history) In 1992, the dollar value of salsa sales in the United States exceeded those of tomato ketchup.
- (about) Since the 2000s sweet salsas combining fruits with peppers like habanero, Scotch bonnet and datil have grown in popularity and are served with frozen dessert, cheesecakes, and pound cakes.
- (about) Not to be confused with sauce, Salsa.

### Seafood

Seafood is any form of sea life regarded as food by humans, prominently including fish and shellfish.

- From: Southwestern US, United States

Fun facts:

- (inspiration) The Dietary Guidelines for Americans recommend eating at least 8 ounces of seafood per week as part of a healthy eating pattern, based on a 2,000-calorie diet.
- (about) The harvesting of wild seafood is usually known as fishing or hunting, while the cultivation and farming of seafood is known as aquaculture and fish farming (in the case of fish).
- (history) A stele of the late 3rd century BCE from the small Boeotian city of Akraiphia, on Lake Copais, provides us with a list of fish prices.

More fun facts (when the user asks for more):

- (culture) Lake Copais itself was famous in all of Greece for its eels, celebrated by the hero of The Acharnians.
